- A piece on the 1st rank? (Bishop at a1).

- A piece on the a1-h8 diagonal? (Bishop at a1).

If king moves to f1, he is still on the 1st rank. Still checked by the rook at a1. (Wait, so e1f1 and e1d1 would be illegal if a rook is at a1).
If king moves to d1, he is still on the 1st rank. Still checked.

So the Rook-at-a1 theory is wrong.

a5, b4, c3, d2, e1.

YES! The Black Queen at a5 is checking the White King at e1 along the diagonal a5-e1.

- e1f1: King moves to f1. (Out of the diagonal). LEGAL.
- e1d1: King moves to d1. (Out of the diagonal). LEGAL.
- c1d2: Bishop moves to d2. (Blocks the diagonal a5-e1). LEGAL.

This perfectly explains all the legal moves!
Now, which move is best?
White is in check. The options are:
1. e1e2
2. e1f1
3. e1d1
4. c1d2

Comparing the moves:
- e1e2: Moves the king to e2. This is generally not ideal as it moves the king towards the center and potentially exposes him to more attacks.
- e1f1: Moves the king to f1. This is a safer square.
- e1d1: Moves the king to d1. This is also a safer square, but might block the bishop or other pieces.
- c1d2: Blocks the check by moving the bishop to d2. This develops the bishop and keeps the king at e1 (where he might be able to castle later, though the rook at h1 is still there, the white king is at e1).

Actually, c1d2 is a very strong move. It blocks the check and develops the piece.

- Queen at a5: Attacks d2. If White plays c1d2, the Black Queen can just take the bishop (Qxd2+).

- Bishop at a1: a1-b2-c3-d4. No.
- Other pieces: No.

That makes c1d2 a bad move.
